========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve just noticed over the past year or so that I don’t seem to know how to sit still anymore. I mean, I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be slowing down a bit, and in some ways of course I have, but I’m always up doing something—puttering around the house, fixing little things that probably don’t need fixing right that minute, rearranging this or that, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing when I maybe ought to just stay home and rest.

It’s not anything dramatic, exactly, but it’s this constant feeling like I should be moving or doing. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s been enough that I thought, well, maybe I should talk to someone about it, because it does feel like I have a hard time just being still.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.

I’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1, F43.2, F32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F06.3, F06.7, F90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve just noticed over the past year or so that I don’t seem to know how to sit still anymore. I mean, I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be slowing down a bit, and in some ways of course I have, but I’m always up doing something—puttering around the house, fixing little things that probably don’t need fixing right that minute, rearranging this or that, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing when I maybe ought to just stay home and rest.\n\nIt’s not anything dramatic, exactly, but it’s this constant feeling like I should be moving or doing. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s been enough that I thought, well, maybe I should talk to someone about it, because it does feel like I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner restlessness or a need to keep moving, even when there’s no specific reason to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.

Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. But no, I haven’t really noticed a lack of energy as the main problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.

If I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.

If I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.

I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. But no, I haven’t really noticed a lack of energy as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.\n\nIf I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. But no, I haven’t really noticed a lack of energy as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.\n\nIf I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. Maybe I can still function, because you sort of make yourself go on with the day, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually energetic or not tired. So no, I can’t really think of a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed that. I mean, there are nights where I might only get a few hours, but I don’t wake up feeling rested from that. I usually feel like I’ve had a short night, even if I get up and do what I need to do.\n\nI wouldn’t say I need less sleep than usual. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, it catches up with me. Sometimes I can keep moving because the day starts whether I’m ready or not, but that’s different from feeling refreshed. So no, I don’t think that’s something that happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy, at least as far as I can tell. At my age, some things are different just because life is different and I don’t always have the same energy or opportunity to do everything I used to do, but that doesn’t feel like the pleasure is gone.\n\nIf something comes up that I normally like, I can still look forward to it. Maybe I’m a little more selective than I used to be, but I haven’t noticed that flat sort of feeling where nothing seems worth doing. So no, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. I mean, my sleep can be a little different from night to night, especially at my age, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of several days where I barely needed sleep and then felt just fine or unusually energetic the next day.

I mean, I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be slowing down a bit, and in some ways of course I have, but I’m always up doing something—puttering around the house, fixing little things that probably don’t need fixing right that minute, rearranging this or that, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing when I maybe ought to just stay home and rest.\n\nIt’s not anything dramatic, exactly, but it’s this constant feeling like I should be moving or doing. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s been enough that I thought, well, maybe I should talk to someone about it, because it does feel like I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner restlessness or a need to keep moving, even when there’s no specific reason to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. But no, I haven’t really noticed a lack of energy as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.\n\nIf I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. Maybe I can still function, because you sort of make yourself go on with the day, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually energetic or not tired. So no, I can’t really think of a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed that. I mean, there are nights where I might only get a few hours, but I don’t wake up feeling rested from that. I usually feel like I’ve had a short night, even if I get up and do what I need to do.\n\nI wouldn’t say I need less sleep than usual. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, it catches up with me. Sometimes I can keep moving because the day starts whether I’m ready or not, but that’s different from feeling refreshed. So no, I don’t think that’s something that happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy, at least as far as I can tell. Nothing where people would say, “What’s gotten into you?” or where I felt especially revved up or euphoric. I mean, I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be slowing down a bit, and in some ways of course I have, but I’m always up doing something—puttering around the house, fixing little things that probably don’t need fixing right that minute, rearranging this or that, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing when I maybe ought to just stay home and rest.\n\nIt’s not anything dramatic, exactly, but it’s this constant feeling like I should be moving or doing. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s been enough that I thought, well, maybe I should talk to someone about it, because it does feel like I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner restlessness or a need to keep moving, even when there’s no specific reason to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.\n\nIf I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. Maybe I can still function, because you sort of make yourself go on with the day, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually energetic or not tired. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. Maybe I can still function, because you sort of make yourself go on with the day, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually energetic or not tired. So no, I can’t really think of a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed that. I mean, there are nights where I might only get a few hours, but I don’t wake up feeling rested from that. I usually feel like I’ve had a short night, even if I get up and do what I need to do.\n\nI wouldn’t say I need less sleep than usual. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, it catches up with me. Sometimes I can keep moving because the day starts whether I’m ready or not, but that’s different from feeling refreshed. So no, I don’t think that’s something that happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy, at least as far as I can tell. At my age, some things are different just because life is different and I don’t always have the same energy or opportunity to do everything I used to do, but that doesn’t feel like the pleasure is gone.\n\nIf something comes up that I normally like, I can still look forward to it. Maybe I’m a little more selective than I used to be, but I haven’t noticed that flat sort of feeling where nothing seems worth doing. So no, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your mood was unusually high, elated, or irritable for several days in a row, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can have moods like anyone else—some days I’m more cheerful, some days I’m more short-tempered, especially if something is frustrating—but I wouldn’t call it unusually high or elated for several days in a row.\n\nAnd with irritability, well, I suppose I can get annoyed about little things sometimes, but it doesn’t seem like a stretch of days where my mood is just not like me. Nothing where people would say, “What’s gotten into you?” or where I felt especially revved up or euphoric. But whether I’m more easily distracted than other people, I don’t really know.\n\nIf I’m trying to focus on something important, I usually try to keep myself with it, though sometimes my attention wanders a bit. But I can’t say I’ve specifically noticed a big problem with being pulled away by every little thing around me. It may happen now and then, but nothing is coming to mind that feels especially unusual or worth pointing to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt energetic and alert during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. I mean, my sleep can be a little different from night to night, especially at my age, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of several days where I barely needed sleep and then felt just fine or unusually energetic the next day.\n\nIf I sleep poorly, I usually notice it. I don’t think so, no—not in a big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, at my age there are things I do less, but that’s not the same as not enjoying them. Sometimes I don’t get around to things, or I’ll put something off, but when I actually do something I like, I can still enjoy it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1, F43.2, F32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F06.3, F06.7, F90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve just noticed over the past year or so that I don’t seem to know how to sit still anymore. I mean, I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be slowing down a bit, and in some ways of course I have, but I’m always up doing something—puttering around the house, fixing little things that probably don’t need fixing right that minute, rearranging this or that, or saying yes to one more volunteer thing when I maybe ought to just stay home and rest.\n\nIt’s not anything dramatic, exactly, but it’s this constant feeling like I should be moving or doing. If I sit down, I’m thinking of the next thing I could straighten or take care of. It’s been enough that I thought, well, maybe I should talk to someone about it, because it does feel like I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner restlessness or a need to keep moving, even when there’s no specific reason to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s hard to describe, but it’s like an ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like if I’m at the senior center and we’re having one of those meetings where people go through announcements, or at church when they’re reading off all the notices, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “All right, just sit quietly,” but my body doesn’t quite cooperate.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, cross and uncross my legs, move my purse, straighten the papers in front of me even if they’re already straight. Sometimes I’ll get up to get coffee, not because I’m especially thirsty, but just because standing up and walking over there gives me something to do for a minute. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or impatient with people. I just get this urge to move, and sitting through it feels much harder than it probably ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel tired or that you don’t have much energy most of the time, even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in the way you mean, I don’t believe. I mean, I’m 87, so of course I get tired more easily than I did when I was younger, and if I’ve had a long day I’ll feel it. But I wouldn’t say I’m tired most of the time, or that resting doesn’t help.\n\nIf I sit for a bit or have a quiet afternoon, I usually feel all right. It’s more that I don’t always settle into the resting very well, if that makes sense. But no, I haven’t really noticed a lack of energy as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. My sleep is… well, at my age it isn’t always one long stretch the way it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I only need a few hours and then feel wonderfully rested or anything like that.\n\nIf I get too little sleep, I generally know it. I feel it the next day. So no, I don’t think there’s been a change where I suddenly need much less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time when you felt so energetic or wired that you didn’t feel like you needed much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can get a little keyed up sometimes, in the ordinary way, like if there’s something I’m thinking about or if the day has been busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt so wired that I didn’t need sleep.\n\nIf I don’t sleep enough, I notice it. I may still be up and moving around because I’m restless or because I have trouble settling, but that’s different from feeling like, “Oh, I only need two or three hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I don’t really have that. I still need my sleep, and when I don’t get it, I pay for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where you felt unusually energetic or excited, and during that time, you felt you could get by on much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe I have. I mean, I’ve certainly had days where I was in better spirits, or where I had something coming up and felt a bit more lively than usual, but not in that way where I was unusually excited and then hardly needed to sleep.\n\nWhen I don’t sleep enough, I tend to notice it. I may get through the day, but I wouldn’t say I feel rested or full of energy on just a little sleep. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or unusually excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had that, not the way you’re describing it. I may have had nights where I slept poorly and still had to get up and carry on the next day, but that’s not the same as feeling completely refreshed.\n\nIf I only get a few hours, I usually feel it one way or another. I might push through because that’s just what you do, especially when there are things to take care of, but I wouldn’t say I feel wonderfully rested or unusually full of energy afterward. So, no, I can’t really think of a time like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so energetic or excited that you felt you hardly needed any sleep at all, and yet still felt completely refreshed the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. Maybe I can still function, because you sort of make yourself go on with the day, but I wouldn’t say I’m unusually energetic or not tired. So no, I can’t really think of a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual or that you feel rested even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed that. I mean, there are nights where I might only get a few hours, but I don’t wake up feeling rested from that. I usually feel like I’ve had a short night, even if I get up and do what I need to do.\n\nI wouldn’t say I need less sleep than usual. If anything, when I don’t sleep enough, it catches up with me. Sometimes I can keep moving because the day starts whether I’m ready or not, but that’s different from feeling refreshed. So no, I don’t think that’s something that happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy, at least as far as I can tell. At my age, some things are different just because life is different and I don’t always have the same energy or opportunity to do everything I used to do, but that doesn’t feel like the pleasure is gone.\n\nIf something comes up that I normally like, I can still look forward to it. Maybe I’m a little more selective than I used to be, but I haven’t noticed that flat sort of feeling where nothing seems worth doing. So no, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your mood was unusually high, elated, or irritable for several days in a row, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can have moods like anyone else—some days I’m more cheerful, some days I’m more short-tempered, especially if something is frustrating—but I wouldn’t call it unusually high or elated for several days in a row.\n\nAnd with irritability, well, I suppose I can get annoyed about little things sometimes, but it doesn’t seem like a stretch of days where my mood is just not like me. Nothing where people would say, “What’s gotten into you?” or where I felt especially revved up or euphoric. So no, I can’t really point to a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I do notice things around me, of course—if there’s a noise in the hallway or the phone rings or someone starts talking nearby, I’ll look up, like anyone would. But whether I’m more easily distracted than other people, I don’t really know.\n\nIf I’m trying to focus on something important, I usually try to keep myself with it, though sometimes my attention wanders a bit. But I can’t say I’ve specifically noticed a big problem with being pulled away by every little thing around me. It may happen now and then, but nothing is coming to mind that feels especially unusual or worth pointing to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt energetic and alert during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. I mean, my sleep can be a little different from night to night, especially at my age, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of several days where I barely needed sleep and then felt just fine or unusually energetic the next day.\n\nIf I sleep poorly, I usually notice it. I know I’ve answered something similar, but I’m trying to think carefully, and I can’t remember any time where I was so energetic or excited that I felt I barely needed sleep and then woke up feeling completely refreshed.\n\nI’ve had ordinary restless nights, of course, or nights where my mind didn’t settle as quickly as I wanted, but if I don’t get enough sleep, I usually know it the next day. I may still get myself going, but I wouldn’t call that feeling refreshed. So no, not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually alert or energetic during the day, even after getting much less sleep than usual, without feeling tired or impaired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that lasting several days. I mean, at my age I’ve had plenty of times where sleep wasn’t perfect, or I woke up earlier than I wanted, or had a day where I was a bit more alert than expected, but not several days of needing much less sleep and then being just fine.\n\nIf I get less sleep than usual, I generally notice it. So no, I don’t think that’s something that happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, no. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy, at least as far as I can tell. At my age, some things are different just because life is different and I don’t always have the same energy or opportunity to do everything I used to do, but that doesn’t feel like the pleasure is gone.\n\nIf something comes up that I normally like, I can still look forward to it. Maybe I’m a little more selective than I used to be, but I haven’t noticed that flat sort of feeling where nothing seems worth doing. So no, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your mood was unusually high, elated, or irritable for several days in a row, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can have moods like anyone else—some days I’m more cheerful, some days I’m more short-tempered, especially if something is frustrating—but I wouldn’t call it unusually high or elated for several days in a row.\n\nAnd with irritability, well, I suppose I can get annoyed about little things sometimes, but it doesn’t seem like a stretch of days where my mood is just not like me. Nothing where people would say, “What’s gotten into you?” or where I felt especially revved up or euphoric. So no, I can’t really point to a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I do notice things around me, of course—if there’s a noise in the hallway or the phone rings or someone starts talking nearby, I’ll look up, like anyone would. But whether I’m more easily distracted than other people, I don’t really know.\n\nIf I’m trying to focus on something important, I usually try to keep myself with it, though sometimes my attention wanders a bit. But I can’t say I’ve specifically noticed a big problem with being pulled away by every little thing around me. It may happen now and then, but nothing is coming to mind that feels especially unusual or worth pointing to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt energetic and alert during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. I mean, my sleep can be a little different from night to night, especially at my age, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of several days where I barely needed sleep and then felt just fine or unusually energetic the next day.\n\nIf I sleep poorly, I usually notice it. I might feel a bit slower or want to rest more. I can’t recall a time where I was sleeping much less than usual and still felt alert and full of energy for days. That doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt you needed much less sleep than usual, and during that time, you still felt energetic and alert during the day?\n\nPatient: Right, I think that’s basically the same thing you were asking just a moment ago, and no, I really can’t say I’ve had that. I’ve had nights where I don’t sleep well, of course, or I wake up earlier than I’d like, but when that happens I usually feel it the next day. I’m not suddenly bright-eyed and energetic on very little sleep.\n\nI don’t remember any period where I only needed a few hours and then felt perfectly alert and full of energy during the day. If I’m tired, I’m tired. So no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt energetic and alert during the day?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it again, and I’m trying to think if there’s some exception I’m missing, but no, I really don’t think so. I’ve had bad nights of sleep here and there, like anybody, where I’m awake too long or I wake up too early, but I don’t feel especially good the next day when that happens.\n\nIf anything, I usually feel worn down or a little off if I haven’t slept enough. I can’t remember a stretch of days where I was sleeping much less than normal and still felt energetic and alert, like I didn’t need the sleep. I don’t think so, no. And if I’m irritable, it’s usually tied to something ordinary, not like a whole stretch where my mood is suddenly changed and noticeable to others. So, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in a big way that I’ve noticed.
